Notice the distinction: a discarded source subset can contain an entire special fibre. Its smaller total dimension, not an assertion that every discarded fibre is finite, justifies induction.

At this fixed source dimension first prove the cases with generic relative dimension zero or one. The zero-dimensional case follows after removing fibre-dimension jumps from the calculation preceding (D.10).

For the curve case choose a stable lattice for the lisse representation on the dense source. Such a lattice exists because its profinite image is compact: the integral span of the translates of any lattice is bounded and still a full lattice. Reduction modulo a sufficiently deep power of the coefficient uniformizer has finite image; its kernel is pro-\(\ell\), by the filtration of congruence subgroups with finite \(\ell\)-group quotients. Take the finite étale cover corresponding to this finite representation quotient. On it the coefficient image lies in that pro-\(\ell\) kernel. Every wild inertia group of a characteristic-\(p\) boundary trait is pro-\(p\); its continuous image in a pro-\(\ell\) group is trivial when \(p\ne\ell\). This kills wild ramification wherever a smooth boundary model is subsequently constructed. Formula (D.10) reduces the desired assertion to the covered coefficient; split its finitely many components if necessary.

Work on its generic curve over \(K=\mathbb F_q(Y_0)\). Over the perfection of \(K\), the reduced curve has a smooth dense open with smooth proper completion, and its finite boundary has separable residue fields. Lemma D.2b supplies this curve model and the finite-presentation descent of its finite étale boundary after a finite purely inseparable extension \(K'/K\). Normalize a normal target open in \(K'\). After shrinking, this is a finite universal homeomorphism. Étale invariance and compact-support base change allow this replacement: they transport actual sheaf filtrations. At finite-field closed points the purely inseparable residue extension is trivial, so Frobenius weights are unchanged.

Spread the finite geometric data of the generic completion, open immersion and boundary over a dense target open. Shrink until the completion is smooth proper and its boundary is finite étale. The generic identification with a dense open of the covered source also spreads after shrinking; the omitted source has dimension less than \(D\). The coefficient is already defined on that source: restrict and pull it back along the identification. No claim that an arbitrary infinite adic system is finitely presented is needed. Its pro-\(\ell\) image makes the extended boundary ramification tame by the preceding inertia argument. Lemma D.1a supplies the relative tame comparison on this smooth pair, from its explicit finite covering input; no additional shrinking is needed for that step. That lemma proves (D.11) on the retained open. The lower-dimensional source and target complements, the universal-homeomorphism equivalence, and the trace retraction recover the entire relative-curve case. Each discarded complement is treated by the outer induction; no arbitrary relative-dimensional case at dimension \(D\) has been used.

Now let the generic relative dimension be \(d\ge2\). Choose \(d-1\) algebraically independent elements of the source function field over the target function field. On a dense source open they define a dominant map to \(\mathbb A_{Y_0}^{d-1}\). Generic flatness and fibre dimension give a nonempty target open \(W_0\) on which this map has pure relative dimension one. Its inverse image is dense; the discarded source again has dimension less than \(D\). On that retained source the original map factors as \[ X'_0\xrightarrow{g}W_0\xrightarrow{h}Y_0. \tag{D.12} \] These maps are separated. The projection \(h\) is separated, so the graph of \(g\) in \(X'_0\times_{Y_0}W_0\) is closed; the projection of that product to \(W_0\) is a base change of the separated original map. Their composite is \(g\). Crucially, \[ \dim W_0=\dim Y_0+d-1=D-1. \tag{D.13} \] The relative-curve case just proved, still allowing source dimension \(D\), makes \(R^bg_!F_0\) mixed of weights at most \(n+b\). Outer induction applies to \(h\), whose source has the smaller dimension in (D.13), and gives \[ R^ah_!\bigl(R^bg_!F_0\bigr) \text{ of weights at most }n+a+b. \tag{D.14} \] These are the terms of compact-support Leray. Each surviving term in total degree \(r\) is a subquotient of one bounded by \(n+r\); the finite abutment filtration preserves mixedness and that bound. No degeneration assertion is required. Localization restores the discarded source and completes induction on \(D\). Finally reassemble the original finite coefficient filtration to obtain (D.11). \(\square\)

For a bounded complex whose ordinary cohomology sheaves have bounds \(w+b\), the ordinary-cohomology spectral sequence for \(Rf_!\) now gives upper bound \(w\) on the resulting complex, exactly as in (4.2). Ordinary inverse image preserves mixedness and weights by the residue-field calculation, and tensor product preserves mixedness by its finite tensor filtration and multiplication of stalk eigenvalues. The remaining operations require a further argument. Appendix E first proves mixedness for ordinary direct image without assuming dual mixedness, and then obtains duality, exceptional inverse image and derived internal Hom. A formal lower inequality expressed using a dual would not by itself establish that stability.

Appendix E. Mixedness of the remaining operations

The compact-support theorem does not establish ordinary direct-image mixedness merely by writing a duality formula: that would already require mixedness of the dual. We first prove ordinary direct-image mixedness without that assumption. Duality is then obtained by stratification, and the other operations follow.

We work in the finite-field constructible characteristic-zero étale category of this lesson. In addition to Appendix D, the ordinary inputs are bounded constructibility and generic base change for ordinary direct image, relative smooth Poincaré duality, finite universal-homeomorphism invariance, finite étale descent, open–closed localization, and constructible Verdier biduality with its functorial exchanges. The geometric inputs include generic smoothness after reduction and finite purely inseparable base change, projective closure of affine schemes, and the dimension formula. Proposition D.2a supplies the radicial coefficient comparison; the paragraph after D.2b identifies AU.6 as the exact proper quasi-finite provider. Lemma E.2a supplies the arbitrary-dimensional smooth-spreading argument with its exact algebraic providers. The operation providers and coefficient identifications listed in Appendix D supply the ordinary constructibility, support, smooth-trace and biduality maps at this full adic scope. Their recursive inputs and the geometric statements remain hypotheses of this deduction. The first proof of Lemma E.2 below does not use generic base change. Proposition E.2b proves the smooth/lisse geometric-fibre comparison for the alternative relative-pairing proof. E.2m below supplies the ordinary constructible generic-base-change theorem; E.2n supplies the uniform completed comparison and its actual rational-sheaf identification. No assertion about preservation of weights by these operations is assumed.

Mixedness can be detected after a finite map

The ordinary subquotient, extension and stratification arguments in Appendix D show that bounded mixed complexes are closed under triangles and ordinary truncations. Mixedness is also local on a finite Zariski open cover. Indeed, partition the union successively into each open's portion not already covered; these are locally closed pieces. Restrict the finite filtrations there and use extension by zero and open–closed gluing. The filtrations on overlapping opens need not coincide.

Lemma E.1. If \(b:T_0\to S_0\) is finite and \(b_*H\) is mixed, then the ordinary sheaf \(H\) is mixed. Consequently finite pushforward detects mixedness of bounded complexes.

Proof. For a finite étale map, \(H\) is a direct summand of \(b^*b_*H\). In the finite étale fibre product \(T_0\times_{S_0}T_0\), the diagonal is both open and closed. The projector onto that component gives the summand, or, on stalks, selects the summand at the given point from the finite fibre sum. Pullback preserves mixedness by the Frobenius-power calculation, so ordinary subquotient closure proves the assertion.

For a general finite map, stratify the reduced base into integral pieces and work first at a generic point. Its finite reduced source algebra is a product of finite field extensions. After a finite purely inseparable extension of the base field, the reductions of these algebras are separable. This can be seen over the perfection, then descended to a finite stage since the algebras and their separability conditions are finitely presented. Spread that finite radicial change over a normal base open. After shrinking, the reduced finite source is étale over the changed base. Finite base change and invariance of étale topoi under the radicial maps reduce to the first paragraph. At closed finite-field points those maps do not change residue fields, so the equivalence transports pure grades and finite mixed filtrations with their weights.

The omitted closed base has smaller dimension. Finite base change identifies the restricted pushforward there; induction proves mixedness on its inverse image. Open–closed gluing supplies a finite mixed filtration on all of \(H\). For a complex, finite pushforward is exact and commutes with ordinary cohomology, so apply the sheaf assertion to every nonzero cohomology sheaf. \(\square\)

A smooth map over a dense base open

Lemma E.2. Let \(a:V_0\to S_0\) be smooth of pure relative dimension \(d\), with \(S_0\) integral, and let \(L_0\) be a pure lisse sheaf. On a dense open of \(S_0\), the complex \(Ra_*L_0\) is mixed.

A proof using the already constructed operations

Proof of Lemma E.2 without generic base change. We may shrink the integral finite-field base \(S_0\) to its nonempty smooth locus. Write \(s=\dim S_0\); then the smooth source \(V_0\) is smooth over the finite field of pure dimension \(s+d\). This uses only the geometric smooth-locus input already specified, and leaves the lemma's dense-open conclusion unchanged. If \(V_0\) is empty the result is immediate.

Set \(A=Ra_!L_0^\vee\). Appendix D makes \(A\) bounded mixed. For each of its finitely many nonzero cohomology sheaves choose a finite mixed filtration. Constructibility lets us shrink the integral base once more so that every term and every grade in this finite list is lisse: intersect their finitely many dense lisse opens. Formation of \(Ra_!\) commutes with this open restriction by BM.5; ordinary direct image commutes with open restriction by the slice adjunction. We replace \(S_0,V_0\) by these opens. No claim about ordinary direct image under a closed or arbitrary geometric-fibre base change is needed.

Use the rational operation comparisons just specified. Put \(D_TK=R\mathcal Hom(K,\omega_T)\), where \(\omega_T\) is the exceptional inverse image of the rational unit under \(T\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb F_q\). For a smooth \(T\) of dimension \(t\), BV.4 gives \(\omega_T=E_T(t)[2t]\). Finite lisse sheaves are dualizable; their evaluation identifies their double dual with themselves. BV.7, transported by BX.7, consequently gives \[ \begin{gathered} D_{S_0}A\\ \simeq Ra_*D_{V_0}(L_0^\vee),\\ D_{V_0}(L_0^\vee)\\ \simeq L_0(s+d)[2s+2d]. \end{gathered} \tag{E.1a} \] These are the dual-exchange and smooth-orientation maps, so they retain the arithmetic action. Moving the shift and the Tate object through direct image is the projection comparison for an invertible rank-one coefficient object; equivalently it can be checked after trivializing that object and descended through its transition maps. Thus (E.1a), together with the smooth formula for \(\omega_{S_0}\), yields \[ \begin{aligned} Ra_*L_0&\simeq B(-d)[-2d],\\ B&=R\mathcal Hom(A,E_{S_0}). \end{aligned} \tag{E.1b} \] In particular the base-dimension twist and shift cancel. This is a statement about the actual derived image, before taking cohomology, not an identification inferred from a fibre calculation.

Here is the cohomology calculation in (E.1b). If \(H\) is finite lisse, then \(R\mathcal Hom(H,E_{S_0})=H^\vee\) in degree zero: locally \(H\) is finite free, where internal Hom is a finite direct sum of the identity functor and is exact, and these identifications glue by the dual transition maps. Apply this to the cohomology sheaves of \(A\), using its finite ordinary truncation tower. The resulting internal-Hom cohomology spectral sequence has only its degree-zero internal-Ext row. It gives the natural identification \[ \begin{gathered} \mathcal H^r(Ra_*L_0)\\ \simeq\bigl(\mathcal H^{\,2d-r}(A)\bigr)^\vee(-d). \end{gathered} \tag{E.1c} \] This also proves that the ordinary-image cohomology sheaves are lisse on the chosen open; their lissity was not an additional assumption. The finite-tower calculation does not assert a general equivalence with the derived category of the constructible heart.

For completeness let \(0=F_0\subset F_1\subset\cdots\subset F_m=H\) be one of the chosen lisse mixed filtrations of \(H=\mathcal H^{2d-r}(A)\). The filtration on \(H^\vee\) is explicitly \[ \begin{gathered} G_i=\ker(H^\vee\longrightarrow F_{m-i}^\vee),\\ G_i/G_{i-1}\\ \simeq(F_{m-i+1}/F_{m-i})^\vee. \end{gathered} \tag{E.1d} \] The first formula is for \(0\leq i\leq m\), and the second for \(1\leq i\leq m\). Restriction to a lisse subrepresentation is surjective on dual vector spaces, hence is an epimorphism of these lisse sheaves. This proves the displayed graded identification and exactness, without a semisimplicity assumption. Dualizing a pure lisse grade inverts its Frobenius eigenvalues and negates its weight; twisting by \((-d)\) adds \(2d\). Formula (E.1c) therefore supplies an actual finite mixed filtration for every \(R^ra_*L_0\). The inverse filtration works equally for zero sheaves, and only finitely many degrees occur by the ordinary-image bound. This proves the full assertion of Lemma E.2.

The only dual-mixedness fact used in this proof is the explicit dual filtration (E.1d) for lisse sheaves. We have not assumed Theorem E.4, general mixed stability of Verdier duality, or generic base change. The relative-pairing proof below uses the actual fibre comparison supplied by Proposition E.2b. \(\square\)

The actual fibre comparison for the relative-pairing proof

Proposition E.2b (smooth source and lisse coefficients). Let \(a:V\to S\) be a separated smooth morphism of pure relative dimension \(d\) between finite-type schemes over a finite field, with \(S\) integral. Let \(E\) be a finite extension of \(\mathbb Q_\ell\), where \(\ell\) is invertible on \(S\), and let \(L\) be a finite-rank lisse \(E\)-sheaf on \(V\). There is a dense open \(U\subset S\) such that every \(R^ra_*L|_U\) is lisse and, for every geometric point \(g:\bar s=\operatorname{Spec}\Omega\to U\), the canonical map \[ \begin{gathered} \beta_g:g^*Ra_*L\longrightarrow Ra_{\bar s,*}L_{\bar s},\\ (R^ra_*L)_{\bar s}\longrightarrow H^r(V_{\bar s},L_{\bar s}) \end{gathered} \tag{E.F1} \] is an isomorphism, in the derived category and on cohomology, respectively. Here the morphism and the sheaf on the right are the pullbacks to \(\bar s\), and on the left \(a\) has been restricted over \(U\). The algebraically closed field \(\Omega\) need not be finite over a residue field. No purity, mixedness, semisimplicity or smoothness of \(S\) is assumed. This proposition proves the comparison used in the following proof. It does not state generic base change for arbitrary morphisms and arbitrary constructible coefficients.

Proof: relative duality before taking a fibre. All operations below are the actual rational constructible operations identified in Constructible complexes, BX.7. Put \(A=Ra_!L^\vee\), \(B=Ra_*L\), and \(A^\vee=R\mathcal Hom_E(A,E_S)\). Bounded constructibility is supplied by BV.1 and BM.6 there. Applying the internal exceptional adjunction (BV.22) to \(K=L^\vee\) and \(G=E_S\), and using the smooth orientation (BV.15), gives the particular map \[ \lambda:B\xrightarrow{\sim}A^\vee(-d)[-2d]. \tag{E.F2} \] Indeed \(a^!E_S=E_V(d)[2d]\), while \(R\mathcal Hom_E(L^\vee,E_V)=L\): on a pro-étale trivialization this is finite-dimensional double evaluation, with its dual transition maps. Thus (BV.22) identifies \(B(d)[2d]\) with \(A^\vee\). Moving the invertible Tate line through ordinary direct image is its projection comparison. No dualizing complex for a smooth base is needed.

Specify the pairing that defines \(\lambda\). In the following display \(\otimes\) denotes derived tensor over the indicated coefficient sheaf, \(\varepsilon:a^*Ra_*L\to L\) is the ordinary adjunction counit, and the last arrow is the positive smooth trace with its twist and shift removed: \[ \begin{aligned} B\otimes A &\longrightarrow Ra_!(a^*B\otimes L^\vee)\\ &\xrightarrow{Ra_!(\varepsilon\otimes1)}Ra_!(L\otimes L^\vee)\\ &\xrightarrow{Ra_!(\mathrm{ev})}Ra_!E_V \xrightarrow{\mathrm{Tr}_a}E_S(-d)[-2d]. \end{aligned} \tag{E.F3} \] The first arrow is the proper-support projection isomorphism. BV.7 proves that (BV.22) is obtained by transposing this evaluation through the specified exceptional counit; consequently (E.F2) is the transpose of (E.F3), not an arbitrary isomorphism between its source and target.

Proof: a dense open and dual pullback. Only finitely many cohomology sheaves of \(A\) occur. For each choose a dense open of the integral base on which it is lisse, and intersect these opens to obtain \(U\). Restriction of both images to this open is the usual slice restriction; for proper support it is also BM.5. On \(U\), every \(\mathcal H^q(A)\) is locally finite free in the pro-étale topology. The evaluation-defined map \[ \kappa_g:g^*A^\vee\longrightarrow R\mathcal Hom_E(g^*A,E_{\bar s}) \tag{E.F4} \] is an isomorphism. Here \(g^*\) is derived ringed inverse image, including the coefficient map, not an unmentioned identification of coefficient sheaves. For a shifted finite lisse sheaf, pull back its trivializing cover: both sides are the same finite dual module with the same transition maps, and (E.F4) is its identity comparison. Both sides are contravariantly exact in \(A\). Apply the finite ordinary truncation triangles of \(A\); induction through this finite tower proves the assertion for \(A\). This argument neither takes an infinite limit of duals nor assumes realization from the derived category of the constructible heart. The same tower, before pullback, gives \[ \mathcal H^r(B)\simeq \bigl(\mathcal H^{2d-r}(A)\bigr)^\vee(-d), \tag{E.F5} \] so these sheaves are lisse. This follows because internal Hom from a finite lisse sheaf is exact locally; the finite internal-Ext spectral sequence has only its degree-zero row.

Proof: identify the canonical map. Write \(g_V:V_{\bar s}\to V\) for the other projection, and set \(A_{\bar s}=Ra_{\bar s,!}L_{\bar s}^\vee\) and \(B_{\bar s}=Ra_{\bar s,*}L_{\bar s}\). The compact-support comparison is \[ \alpha_g:g^*A\xrightarrow{\sim}A_{\bar s}. \tag{E.F6} \] BM.5 constructs this map for arbitrary base change, explicitly including bases not of finite type over the original field. Its proper compactification and quasi-compact open pull back to \(\bar s\). For rational coefficients the same map is obtained from that completed comparison by localization: BW.7 preserves the comparison, and BX.3 and BX.7 identify inverse image and proper support with their actual ringed functors. The local finite-complex inverse-image calculation and the quasi-compact-slice localization calculation used there apply also to \(g\). On the fibre, apply BV and BX over the algebraically closed field \(\Omega\), where \(V_{\bar s}\) is of finite type. This gives \(\lambda_{\bar s}:B_{\bar s}\xrightarrow{\sim}A_{\bar s}^\vee(-d)[-2d]\).

Define the map on duals in the required direction by \[ \begin{gathered} \delta_g:g^*A^\vee\longrightarrow A_{\bar s}^\vee,\\ \delta_g=R\mathcal Hom_E(\alpha_g^{-1},E_{\bar s})\circ\kappa_g. \end{gathered} \tag{E.F7} \] It is an isomorphism. The inverse in this formula is essential: duality reverses the arrow \(\alpha_g\).

The ordinary comparison \(\beta_g\) is defined by the ordinary adjunction \(a_{\bar s}^*\dashv Ra_{\bar s,*}\). Its transpose is the map \(a_{\bar s}^*g^*B=g_V^*a^*B\xrightarrow{g_V^*\varepsilon}L_{\bar s}\). In particular, with \(\varepsilon_{\bar s}\) the fibre counit, \[ \varepsilon_{\bar s}\circ a_{\bar s}^*\beta_g =g_V^*\varepsilon. \tag{E.F8} \] Both sides are maps \(a_{\bar s}^*g^*B\to L_{\bar s}\). Expand (E.F3) on the base and on the fibre. The projection comparisons commute with (E.F6), since they are induced by the same tensor adjunction and support maps. Their next arrows commute by (E.F8); evaluation commutes with pullback of a finite lisse dual. Finally the trace commutes with this base change. The latter is the coordinate-trace assertion of BJ.6: coordinate covers, the affine-space positive trace and the étale summation trace all pull back, including to geometric fields. BV.2–BV.4 construct the compatible coefficient-diagram counits from those traces; BW.7 and BX.7 preserve these maps. Thus this use requires no exceptional adjoint on an arbitrary scheme outside their field scope.

Consequently, if \(p\) and \(p_{\bar s}\) denote the two pairings (E.F3), then \(p_{\bar s}\circ(\beta_g\otimes\alpha_g)=g^*p\), with the canonical tensor and Tate identifications. Transposing this equality, and using the definition (E.F4), proves the equality of actual morphisms \[ \lambda_{\bar s}\circ\beta_g =\delta_g(-d)[-2d]\circ g^*\lambda. \tag{E.F9} \] The three maps other than \(\beta_g\) in this equality are isomorphisms. Therefore \(\beta_g\) itself is an isomorphism. Inverse image is exact on constructible rational sheaves (BQ and BX.3), so cohomology gives exactly the second map in (E.F1). The construction uses only canonical evaluation, counits and the fixed positive trace, and hence also retains any arithmetic action carried by the original sheaves. For the empty source all objects and maps are zero; for \(d=0\) the trace is the same étale summation trace. Unequal relative dimensions are treated on the finitely many open-and-closed relative-dimension pieces, not identified with one another. \(\square\)

E.2m and E.2n below supply the broader ordinary and adic/rational generic-base-change statements; E.2b remains an independent smooth/lisse proof rather than their substitute. It supplies the smooth/lisse instance, including the canonical geometric-fibre map, used in the relative-pairing argument below.

Recovering comparison on a finite residual locus

The general generic-base-change argument must eventually pass from a comparison on an open set to a comparison on the whole target. Here is the precise detection step. It does not require knowing in advance that the unknown direct-image complex is constructible. We first state it for ordinary étale sheaves of modules over a fixed commutative ring \(\Lambda\); an adic or rational application needs its own coefficient comparison.

Lemma E.2c (finite residual detection). Let \(b:Y\to S\) be a morphism of schemes, \(i:Z\hookrightarrow Y\) a closed immersion, and \(u:W=Y\setminus Z\hookrightarrow Y\) its open complement. Suppose \(q=b i:Z\to S\) is finite. For a morphism \[ \alpha:M\longrightarrow N,\qquad M,N\in D^+(Y_{\mathrm{\acute et}},\Lambda), \tag{E.R1} \] assume that both \(u^*\alpha\) and \(Rb_*\alpha\) are isomorphisms. Then \(\alpha\) is an isomorphism. Neither constructibility of \(M,N\) nor properness of \(b\) is an additional hypothesis of this lemma.

Proof: why the finite direct image detects a map. Finite pushforward, Theorem 4.1 proves exactness and the natural geometric-stalk formula \[ (q_*G)_{\bar s}=\prod_{\bar z\mid\bar s}G_{\bar z}, \qquad R^rq_*G=0\quad(r>0). \tag{E.R2} \] The same formulas hold for \(\Lambda\)-modules: the restrictions defining them respect the coefficient action. The finite set indexes the geometric lifts with one fixed algebraically closed field, not chosen isomorphism classes of stalks. Every geometric point of \(Z\) is a lift of its image in \(S\). Consequently \(q_*G=0\) implies that every stalk of \(G\) is zero, so \(G=0\). Surjectivity of \(q\) is unnecessary. An empty fibre contributes the zero module and loses no point upstairs.

Exactness gives \(\mathcal H^r(Rq_*K)=q_*\mathcal H^r(K)\) for a bounded-below complex \(K\). Thus \(Rq_*K=0\) implies \(K=0\). Applying this conclusion to a cone shows that \(Rq_*\) reflects isomorphisms. This is a property of the finite morphism \(q\), not an assertion that an arbitrary proper direct image is conservative.

Put \(C=\operatorname{Cone}(\alpha)\). Inverse images and extension by zero for the open and closed immersions are exact. Localization, Proposition 5.2 therefore gives the natural triangle \[ u_!u^*C\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow i_*i^*C \longrightarrow (u_!u^*C)[1]. \tag{E.R3} \] It follows by applying the sheaf-level short exact sequence to a representative complex; exact functors preserve its quasi-isomorphisms. The first assumption gives \(u^*C=0\), hence the displayed map identifies \(C\) with \(i_*i^*C\). The second assumption gives \(Rb_*C=0\).

There is a natural composition identification \(Rb_*i_*K=Rq_*K\). Indeed \(i_*\) is exact by (E.R2), and it sends injectives to injectives because its left adjoint \(i^*\) is exact. An injective resolution of \(K\) thus computes both sides, using the actual equality \(b_*i_*=q_*\). With \(K=i^*C\) we obtain \[ 0=Rb_*C=Rq_*i^*C. \tag{E.R4} \] Finite detection gives \(i^*C=0\), and (E.R3) then gives \(C=0\). Thus the original morphism \(\alpha\), rather than some independently chosen comparison of its endpoints, is invertible. \(\square\)

Corollary E.2d (the actual base-change map). Let \(f:X\to Y\), let \(a=b f:X\to S\), and let \(F\in D^+(X_{\mathrm{\acute et}},\Lambda)\). For any \(g:T\to S\), form the Cartesian diagrams, with the indicated projections: \[ \begin{gathered} X_T\xrightarrow{f_T}Y_T\xrightarrow{b_T}T,\qquad X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{b}S,\\ h_X:X_T\to X,\quad h_Y:Y_T\to Y,\quad a_T=b_Tf_T,\\ Z_T=Z\times_ST,\quad W_T=W\times_ST. \end{gathered} \tag{E.R5} \] Retain the hypothesis that \(Z\to S\) is finite. Assume the following three particular maps are isomorphisms:

  1. The restriction to \(W_T\) of \(\beta_{f,g}:h_Y^*Rf_*F\to Rf_{T*}h_X^*F\).
  2. The canonical comparison \(\gamma_{b,g}:g^*Rb_*Rf_*F\to Rb_{T*}h_Y^*Rf_*F\).
  3. The canonical comparison \(\beta_{a,g}:g^*Ra_*F\to Ra_{T*}h_X^*F\).

Then \(\beta_{f,g}\) itself is an isomorphism everywhere on \(Y_T\).

Proof: compatibility under composition. Use the composition identifications for ordinary derived direct images. They are computed by injective resolutions because direct image preserves injectives, its inverse-image left adjoint being exact. The comparison maps satisfy \[ \beta_{a,g} = Rb_{T*}(\beta_{f,g})\circ\gamma_{b,g}. \tag{E.R6} \] Here both sides go from \(g^*Rb_*Rf_*F\) to \(Rb_{T*}Rf_{T*}h_X^*F\). To verify equality rather than merely compare these objects, recall the defining adjunction transpose of ordinary base change. For \(f\), this is \[ f_T^*h_Y^*Rf_*F =h_X^*f^*Rf_*F \xrightarrow{h_X^*\varepsilon_f}h_X^*F, \tag{E.R7} \] where \(\varepsilon_f:f^*Rf_*\to\mathrm{id}\) is the derived counit. Transpose the right side of (E.R6) successively across \(b_T^*\dashv Rb_{T*}\) and \(f_T^*\dashv Rf_{T*}\). Naturality of the latter comparison and the defining transpose of \(\gamma_{b,g}\) give, in order, the pullback of \(f^*\varepsilon_b\) followed by the pullback of \(\varepsilon_f\). Their composite is the counit of the composed adjunction \(a^*=f^*b^*\dashv Rb_*Rf_*=Ra_*\). This is precisely the transpose defining \(\beta_{a,g}\); uniqueness of adjunction transposes proves (E.R6).

Assumptions 2 and 3 now imply that \(Rb_{T*}(\beta_{f,g})\) is invertible. The closed complement \(Z_T\to T\) is finite, since it is a base change of \(q\). Apply Lemma E.2c over \(T\) to the morphism \(\beta_{f,g}\), using assumption 1 on \(W_T\). This proves the corollary. \(\square\)

If \(b\) is proper, a proper-base-change theorem at the stated coefficient and boundedness scope can supply assumption 2. It must apply to the actual object \(Rf_*F\); invoking a version restricted to constructible inputs before establishing constructibility of that object would be circular. The corollary deliberately records that comparison as an input, rather than concealing such an assumption.

For the qcqs, bounded-below ordinary torsion case, Proposition E.2g below supplies this input from BE.14, including torsion preservation and compatibility with the coefficient action. It does not assume constructibility of \(Rf_*F\).

This is the finite-residual step in the proof strategy of Deligne, SGA 4½, Théorèmes de finitude, §2.6, printed p.240. The deduction also requires a good open locus, a finite complement, a whole-space comparison and bounded constructibility at the required coefficients. In particular E.2b's geometric-fibre statement is not automatically an arbitrary-base-change statement; the corollary uses it only at that scope. Nor does it pass from torsion levels to adic or rational coefficients by an infinite intersection of dense opens. These lemmas complete the detection argument, not the general generic-base-change theorem.

Lemma E.2h and Proposition E.2i below give the separate constructibility-and-boundedness detection step over Noetherian schemes. They retain explicit whole-space finiteness inputs; invertibility of a comparison map alone does not establish constructibility.

Constructing the finite residual locus by coordinates

Lemma E.2c requires a complement that is finite over the base, not merely a finite set in a generic fibre. We now supply the geometric construction used in the dimension induction. The geometry works over an integral Noetherian base; no smoothness, reducedness, or equality of component dimensions is imposed on the schemes over that base.

Lemma E.2e (coordinate opens and their finite complement). Let \(S\) be integral Noetherian with generic field \(K\), and let \(Y\to S\) be of finite type. On a finite affine cover of \(Y\), after replacing \(S\) by an affine neighbourhood of its generic point, choose closed embeddings \[ Y_a\hookrightarrow\mathbf A^{r_a}_S,\qquad p_{ai}:Y_a\longrightarrow\mathbf A^1_S. \tag{E.G1} \] Suppose that for every chosen coordinate there is a dense open \(V_{ai}\subset\mathbf A^1_S\). Set \[ W=\bigcup_{a,i}p_{ai}^{-1}(V_{ai})\subset Y. \tag{E.G2} \] After one further nonempty open restriction of \(S\), the reduced closed complement \(Z=(Y\setminus W)_{\mathrm{red}}\) is finite over \(S\). A chart needing no generators may be embedded using the extra coordinate zero, so every \(r_a\) can be taken positive.

Proof on an affine chart. Write \(S=\operatorname{Spec}A\). Since \(A\) is a domain, \(\mathbf A^1_S\) is integral and every dense open \(V_{ai}\) contains its generic point. Give its closed complement an ideal \(I_{ai}\subset A[T]\). This ideal contains a nonzero polynomial. Choose one and invert its nonzero leading coefficient in \(A\); after scaling, it is a monic polynomial \[ P_{ai}(T)=T^{d_{ai}}+\sum_{e<d_{ai}}c_{ai,e}T^e\in I_{ai}A_s[T]. \tag{E.G3} \] There are finitely many coordinates and charts, so the same nonzero \(s\in A\) works for all of them. If the polynomial has degree zero, its scaled value is one and that closed complement is empty. Otherwise the quotient \(A_s[T]/I_{ai}A_s[T]\) is generated as an \(A_s\)-module by the powers \(1,T,\ldots,T^{d_{ai}-1}\): division by a monic polynomial uses no further denominators.

For a fixed chart, the complement of its union of coordinate opens has the underlying closed set of \[ C_a=Y_{a,s}\mathbin{\times}_{\mathbf A^{r_a}_{A_s}} \prod_i\operatorname{Spec}(A_s[T_i]/I_{ai}A_s[T_i]). \tag{E.G4} \] The product is over \(\operatorname{Spec}A_s\). Its algebra is generated as a module by the monomials \(\prod_iT_i^{e_i}\), with \(0\le e_i<d_{ai}\). The embedding of \(Y_a\) makes \(C_a\) a closed subscheme of this finite scheme. Quotienting a finite module is finite, so \(C_a\to\operatorname{Spec}A_s\) is finite, including all its nilpotents. If one factor is empty, \(C_a\) is empty.

Passage from the charts to \(Y\). The open \(W\) in (E.G2) may also contain points supplied by another chart. Thus \(Z\cap Y_{a,s}\) is a reduced closed subscheme of \(C_a\), not necessarily all of \(C_a\). To check the scheme inclusion, every equation of \(C_a\) vanishes at every point of this reduced scheme, hence vanishes in its coordinate sheaf. Each \(Z\cap Y_{a,s}\) is therefore finite over the restricted base.

Finiteness is not, in general, a property that one may glue from opens of the source. Instead inspect the generic fibre. The finitely many opens \((Z\cap Y_{a,s})_K\) are finite \(K\)-schemes. Hence \(Z_K\) has finitely many points, all closed, with finite-dimensional local \(K\)-algebras. Each singleton is open: it is the complement of finitely many closed points. Its scheme structure is an open-and-closed component of one of those finite affine charts, so it is affine and finite over \(K\). Their finite disjoint union is affine and finite. This proves that \(Z_K\to\operatorname{Spec}K\) is finite.

Now apply Limits of schemes, Theorem 4.2 to the inverse system \(\operatorname{Spec}A_{st}\), \(0\ne t\in A\), whose limit is \(\operatorname{Spec}K\). The model \(Z\) is of finite presentation: it is a closed subscheme of a finite-type scheme over a Noetherian base. The transition maps are affine, and the models are quasi-compact and quasi-separated. These are exactly the theorem's hypotheses. Its proof first descends affineness by Lemma 2.3 and then descends a finite multiplication table of module generators. It consequently makes the actual morphism \(Z_{A_{st}}\to\operatorname{Spec}A_{st}\) finite for one nonzero \(t\). This supplies the required common shrinking; it is not an appeal to generic dimension alone. \(\square\)

The shrinking is necessary. For \(A=k[s]\), the closed subscheme \(V(sT-1)\subset\mathbf A^1_A\) has algebra \(A[1/s]\). Over \(D(s)\) it has the monic equation \(T-1/s=0\), but it is not finite over \(A\). Otherwise \(1/s\) would satisfy a monic equation; multiplying that equation by the appropriate power of \(s\) would give \(1\in(s)\), a contradiction. The empty fibre at \(s=0\) does not repair that failure.

Corollary E.2f (the lower-dimensional induction supplies the opens). Fix \(n\ge1\), a dense open immersion \(j:X\hookrightarrow Y\) of finite-type \(S\)-schemes, and a constructible torsion \(\Lambda\)-sheaf \(F\), with its annihilating integer invertible on \(S\). Assume \(\dim X_K\le n\). Use the same coefficient hypotheses throughout the following induction assumption: for every integral Noetherian base and every such dense open immersion with generic source dimension at most \(n-1\), there is a dense base open over which all ordinary higher direct images are constructible, vanish outside a finite range, and commute with every base change. This assumption is the lower-dimensional theorem, not its geometric-fibre variant.

Then, after shrinking \(S\), there is an open \(W\subset Y\) with finite closed complement over \(S\), such that the conclusions just listed hold for \(Rj_*F\) on \(W\). For each \(g:T\to S\), this includes the restriction of the actual canonical map \[ h_Y^*Rj_*F\longrightarrow Rj_{T*}h_X^*F \tag{E.G5} \] to \(W_T=W\times_ST\), where \(h_X:X_T\to X\) and \(h_Y:Y_T\to Y\) are the projections. It does not yet assert that (E.G5) is invertible on the finite complement.

Proof: the dimension decrease for each coordinate. On a chart in (E.G1), write \(X_a=X\cap Y_a\). The generic point of the new base \(\mathbf A^1_S\) has field \(K(T)\). For an irreducible component of \((X_a)_K\), with function field \(L\), there are two cases. If the coordinate \(p_{ai}\) is algebraic over \(K\) in \(L\), some nonzero polynomial in \(K[T]\) becomes zero, so this component has empty fibre over \(K(T)\). If it is transcendental, then \[ \operatorname{trdeg}_{K(T)}L=\operatorname{trdeg}_K L-1\le n-1. \tag{E.G6} \] The equality follows by adjoining the coordinate to a transcendence basis and applying exchange. The dimension/transcendence-degree calculation is the monic-normalization proof in Constructible complexes, K.1 and M.1; its algebraic part applies over arbitrary fields. It identifies the dimension of the nonempty generic component with the left side of (E.G6). Nilpotents do not change prime chains. Taking the maximum over the finitely many surviving components proves the bound without assuming equal dimensions. An empty generic source is harmless.

Here “algebraic” must not be replaced by “a scalar in \(K\)”. For example, over \(\mathbf Q\), the coordinate \(x\) on \(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbf Q[x,y]/(x^2-2)\) is algebraic but not rational constant. Its generic fibre over the \(x\)-line is empty, since \(T^2-2\) is inverted in \(\mathbf Q(T)\). The projection to the \(y\)-line instead has a zero-dimensional generic fibre. Both fall within the two cases above.

The restriction \(X_a\hookrightarrow Y_a\), now viewed over \(\mathbf A^1_S\) through \(p_{ai}\), is still an open immersion between finite-type schemes. Its open image is dense; localization to a generic fibre retains the generic points of the components that survive. The lower-dimensional induction assumption supplies a dense open \(V_{ai}\) of that base. Lemma E.2e then makes the complement of the union (E.G2) finite after a common shrinking.

Proof: why this gives the required comparison on \(W\). Restriction of ordinary derived direct image to an open of its target is the direct image for the restricted morphism. At sheaf level the two functors have the same sections on every etale open. At derived level the target-open restriction preserves injectives, since its left adjoint is the exact extension by zero; the corresponding source-open restriction does too. Thus an injective resolution proves the identity, and the counits identify the canonical comparisons, not merely their endpoints.

For an arbitrary \(T\to S\), apply the induction conclusion over \(V_{ai}\) to the base change \[ V_{ai}\times_ST\longrightarrow V_{ai}. \tag{E.G7} \] The resulting Cartesian source and target are exactly the original ones restricted to \(p_{ai}^{-1}(V_{ai})\times_ST\). Consequently its comparison is the restriction of (E.G5), by the just-described counit identity. Those opens cover \(W_T\). A morphism of sheaf complexes is an isomorphism if it is so on this cover, because the cohomology sheaves of its cone then vanish on every open in the cover. Constructibility is also local on a finite open cover: intersect the finitely many strata and opens to form one finite locally closed stratification. Taking the maximum of the finitely many cohomological bounds supplies a common finite bound on \(W\). This proves the stated conclusions. \(\square\)

This expands the coordinate reduction in Deligne, SGA 4½, Théorèmes de finitude, Lemma 2.4, printed pp.239–240. In the general generic-base-change proof, E.2f supplies the good-open input of E.2d, and E.2e supplies its finite complement. The lower-dimensional induction hypothesis, the whole-space comparison, constructibility across that complement, and the coefficient passage are the subsequent steps of the deduction. These results complete the geometric reduction, not the general theorem; geometric-fibre comparison alone does not establish arbitrary base change.

Proper comparison before constructibility

The finite-residual argument must apply proper base change to the actual direct image, not to a constructible replacement whose existence is still to be proved. The needed ordinary-coefficient statement follows from the earlier proof with no constructibility hypothesis.

Proposition E.2g (the proper comparison input). Let \(f:X\to Y\) be quasi-compact and quasi-separated, let \(b:Y\to S\) be proper, and fix a commutative coefficient ring \(\Lambda\). Let \(F\in D^+(X_{\mathrm{\acute et}},\Lambda)\), and assume that the underlying abelian sheaf of each \(\mathcal H^r(F)\) is torsion. Then \(Rf_*F\) is bounded below and has torsion underlying cohomology sheaves. For every morphism \(g:T\to S\), the canonical map in assumption 2 of Corollary E.2d is an isomorphism: \[ \gamma_{b,g}:g^*Rb_*Rf_*F \xrightarrow{\sim}Rb_{T*}h_Y^*Rf_*F. \tag{E.P1} \] Neither constructibility of \(Rf_*F\), a finite upper cohomological bound, nor a common annihilator of all its cohomology sheaves is assumed. The proposition concerns ordinary sheaf complexes; it asserts no adic or rational comparison.

Proof: comparing coefficients without assuming that forgetting preserves injectives. The argument in Constructible complexes, BB.3 is stated for \(\mathbb F_\ell\), but its acyclicity argument works for the present \(\Lambda\). Here are the required details. On a qcqs scheme, take the finite-family hypercovers used in BA.5. The augmented complex of free \(\Lambda\)-module sheaves is exact: the local finite-support filling contracts a cycle by the identity \(\partial c+c\partial=1\), with coefficients in \(\Lambda\). This uses the explicit filling, not a claim that tensoring an arbitrary exact integral complex preserves exactness. Applying \(\operatorname{Hom}_\Lambda(-,J)\), for a module-injective sheaf \(J\), therefore gives an exact augmented cochain complex. Its terms are the products of the section groups of \(J\) on the hypercover objects. The hypercover computation in BA.5 consequently gives \[ H^q(U,J)=0\qquad(q>0) \tag{E.P2} \] for abelian-sheaf cohomology on a qcqs scheme \(U\).

Restriction to an étale object preserves module injectives. Indeed, its extension-by-zero left adjoint has stalks equal to direct sums over geometric lifts; these sums are exact, and geometric stalks detect exactness. This is the slice argument in AB.3, with the coefficient actions retained. If \(v:V\to W\) is qcqs and \(U\to W\) is affine étale, then \(V_U\) is qcqs. Applying (E.P2) after restriction, and the sheafification formula (AB.8), shows that a module-injective sheaf on \(V\) is acyclic for the underlying abelian direct image by \(v\). Thus a bounded-below module-injective resolution computes both derived direct images. The acyclic-resolution comparison is the identity in degree zero and commutes with restriction, connecting maps and coefficient endomorphisms. In particular it preserves the counits defining base change. We have not asserted that the underlying abelian sheaf of a module injective is injective.

Proof: torsion survives a qcqs direct image. First let \(G\) be a torsion abelian sheaf on \(X\), and put \(G_m=\ker(m!:G\to G)\) for \(m\geq1\). On every geometric stalk, every element of finite order belongs to some \(G_m\), so \(G=\varinjlim_m G_m\). For affine étale \(U\to Y\), the scheme \(X_U\) is qcqs. The fixed-scheme filtered-coefficient consequence of BA.5 gives continuity of every \(H^q(X_U,-)\). Using (AB.8) and exact sheafification yields \[ R^qf_*G\simeq\varinjlim_m R^qf_*G_m \qquad(q\geq0). \tag{E.P3} \] Multiplication by \(m!\) is the zero morphism of \(G_m\), and additive derived functors carry it to the zero morphism of \(R^qf_*G_m\). Each term on the right is therefore killed by \(m!\). Filtered colimits have the corresponding colimits as their geometric stalks; each element there comes from a torsion term. This proves torsion, without claiming one integer annihilates the colimit.

Now let \(K\) be the underlying bounded-below complex of \(F\). For a complex whose cohomology lies in a finite interval, the truncation triangles with quotients \(\mathcal H^r(K)[-r]\) and their long cohomology sequences show that all cohomology sheaves of \(Rf_*K\) are torsion. The reason is that torsion abelian groups are closed under subquotients and extensions: if an element maps to one killed by \(n\), its multiple by \(n\) lies in the kernel and is killed by some \(m\), so the original element is killed by \(mn\).

For the general bounded-below case, fix any integer \(d\). The complex \(\tau_{\leq d}K\) has cohomology in a finite interval (or is zero), while \(\tau_{\geq d+1}K\) admits a bounded-below injective resolution beginning in degree \(d+1\). Applying direct image leaves that resolution zero in lower degrees. The truncation triangle consequently identifies \[ \mathcal H^q(Rf_*\tau_{\leq d}K) \simeq\mathcal H^q(Rf_*K) \qquad(q\leq d). \tag{E.P4} \] The finite-interval argument proves torsion on the left. Since \(d\) is arbitrary, it proves torsion in every degree on the right. The same resolution argument preserves the original lower bound. The coefficient comparison already proved transfers this conclusion to \(Rf_*F\).

Proof: applying the exact earlier proper theorem. Constructible complexes, BE.7, equation (BE.14) proves proper base change for every bounded-below complex with torsion abelian cohomology, without constructibility and for arbitrary changes of base. Apply it to \(b\) and to the actual underlying complex of \(Rf_*F\), whose hypotheses have just been checked. Proper morphisms, their base changes, and \(f_T\) are qcqs, so the coefficient comparison applies to all direct images involved. Exact inverse images commute with forgetting the coefficient action. The comparison is natural in coefficient endomorphisms and hence is \(\Lambda\)-linear. Both (BE.14) and (E.P1) are defined by the pulled-back derived counit; the resolution identifications preserve this counit. They identify the particular map \(\gamma_{b,g}\), rather than providing an unrelated isomorphism between its endpoints. An isomorphism after forgetting \(\Lambda\) is an isomorphism of \(\Lambda\)-module complexes, since the forgetful functor is exact and conservative. This proves (E.P1). \(\square\)

Application and boundaries. In the finite-type Noetherian situation of E.2f, the open immersion \(j\) is qcqs. Its stated torsion coefficients satisfy E.2g. Whenever the target morphism \(b\) is proper, assumption 2 of E.2d is therefore supplied for \(Rj_*F\), before proving its constructibility. This does not impose torsion or qcqs hypotheses on the more general conditional statement E.2d: outside the scope just proved, its comparison remains an explicit input. It also does not prove assumption 3, the lower-dimensional induction theorem, bounded constructibility across the finite complement, or the adic/rational passage. No shrinking of the base is needed for E.2g itself, and this step imposes no invertibility condition on the torsion primes.

Quasi-compactness cannot simply be dropped from the torsion-preservation argument. Over an algebraically closed field \(k\), take \(X=\coprod_{m\geq1}\operatorname{Spec}k\to\operatorname{Spec}k\) and the sheaf with value \(\mathbf Z/2^m\mathbf Z\) on its \(m\)-th component. It is torsion at every stalk, but its direct image has section group \(\prod_{m\geq1}\mathbf Z/2^m\mathbf Z\). The element with every component equal to \(1\) has infinite order. This example explains the qcqs continuity hypothesis; it is not a counterexample to the proper theorem.

Constructibility and bounds on the finite residual locus

Finite pushforward does more than detect a zero complex. The additional constructibility argument uses the Noetherian quotient theorem, not an assumption that the unknown sheaf is already constructible.

Lemma E.2h (finite pushforward reflects bounded constructibility). Let \(q:Z\to S\) be finite, with \(S\) Noetherian, and let \(\Lambda\) be a commutative Noetherian ring. Use the module convention: constructible means finitely generated locally constant on a finite locally closed partition. For an arbitrary \(\Lambda\)-module sheaf \(G\) on \(Z\), \[ G\text{ is constructible}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad q_*G\text{ is constructible}. \tag{E.C1} \] For \(L\in D^+(Z_{\mathrm{\acute et}},\Lambda)\), this gives \[ L\in D^b_c(Z,\Lambda)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad Rq_*L\in D^b_c(S,\Lambda). \tag{E.C2} \] Moreover, for each degree \(r\), \(\mathcal H^r(L)=0\) if and only if \(\mathcal H^r(Rq_*L)=0\). Thus the same cohomological interval works on both sides. Surjectivity of \(q\) is not required. The finite-stalk torsion-abelian convention satisfies the corresponding assertions; for finite \(\Lambda\) the two conventions coincide.

Proof. The finite scheme \(Z\) is Noetherian, and \(q\) is of finite presentation: on a Noetherian affine base its finite algebra has a finite list of algebra generators and a finitely generated relation ideal. The forward direction of (E.C1) is therefore Constructible sheaves and extension by zero, Theorem 6.1. Its proof uses finite-algebra splitting and Noetherian induction, independently of a later higher-direct-image constructibility theorem.

For the converse, pullback permanence in the same lesson, Proposition 3.2 makes \(q^*q_*G\) constructible. The adjunction counit \[ q^*q_*G\longrightarrow G \tag{E.C3} \] is surjective. Indeed, at a geometric point \(\bar z\) with image \(\bar s\), the canonical finite-stalk formula (E.R2) identifies it with the projection from the product over all lifts of \(\bar s\) to the factor \(G_{\bar z}\). That projection is surjective; for an actual point \(\bar z\), the indexing set is not empty. Geometric stalks detect epimorphisms. Proposition 7.1 of that lesson proves that every quotient of a constructible module sheaf on a Noetherian scheme is constructible, even if its kernel has not yet been shown constructible. Applying this exact result to (E.C3) proves the converse. Merely knowing that maps between constructible sheaves have constructible cokernels would not justify this step.

By Finite pushforward, Theorem 4.1, \(q_*\) is exact. It is also conservative: a nonzero stalk upstairs occurs in the product at its image, regardless of whether \(q\) covers the rest of \(S\). Hence \[ \mathcal H^r(Rq_*L)=q_*\mathcal H^r(L),\qquad q_*\mathcal H^r(L)=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \mathcal H^r(L)=0. \tag{E.C4} \] The sheaf result in every degree and this exact preservation and reflection of vanishing prove (E.C2) and the interval assertion. For finitely many nonzero constructible cohomology sheaves, their finitely many partitions have a common finite refinement. No initially unknown upper bound has been assumed. For finite-stalk abelian sheaves, apply the same quotient and pullback results in their abelian version; a common annihilator exists on each finite constructible partition. \(\square\)

Proposition E.2i (the residual constructibility criterion). Let \(b:Y\to S\) be a morphism of Noetherian schemes, let \(i:Z\hookrightarrow Y\) be closed, let \(u:W=Y\setminus Z\hookrightarrow Y\), and suppose \(q=bi\) is finite. With \(\Lambda\) as in E.2h and \(K\in D^+(Y_{\mathrm{\acute et}},\Lambda)\), suppose the following three objects are bounded constructible: \[ C=u^*K,\qquad A=Rb_*u_!C,\qquad B=Rb_*K. \tag{E.C5} \] Then \(K\) is bounded constructible. This statement does not assume that the initially unknown restriction \(i^*K\) is constructible or bounded above, and does not claim that proper direct image alone is conservative.

Proof with explicit degree bounds. The localization triangle is \[ u_!C\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow i_*i^*K \longrightarrow(u_!C)[1]. \tag{E.C6} \] Apply \(Rb_*\). Exactness of \(i_*\) and preservation of injectives, established in E.2c, identify its third image with \(Rq_*i^*K\). Thus we obtain \[ A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow Rq_*i^*K\longrightarrow A[1]. \tag{E.C7} \] The long exact cohomology sequence makes each cohomology sheaf of the third term an extension of a cokernel by a kernel of maps between the constructible cohomology sheaves of \(A\) and \(B\). Proposition 3.2 of the constructible-sheaf lesson proves the required kernel, cokernel and extension permanence over \(\Lambda\). If their cohomology is supported in intervals \([a_0,a_1]\) and \([b_0,b_1]\), respectively, the same sequence gives the interval \[ [z_0,z_1]= [\min(b_0,a_0-1),\ \max(b_1,a_1-1)] \tag{E.C8} \] for \(Rq_*i^*K\). Here the minus one comes from \(A[1]\); it must not be changed to a plus one. Empty cohomology is allowed within any of these bounds.

Lemma E.2h now makes \(i^*K\) constructible with the same interval. Since \(Y\) is Noetherian, the open immersion \(u\) is quasi-compact and of finite presentation; \(i\) is a finite morphism of finite presentation. Theorem 6.1 therefore makes \(u_!C\) and \(i_*i^*K\) bounded constructible. Apply the constructible cohomology argument once more to (E.C6). If \(C\) is supported in \([c_0,c_1]\), it gives \[ K\in D^{[\min(c_0,z_0),\ \max(c_1,z_1)]}(Y,\Lambda) \quad\text{with constructible cohomology}. \tag{E.C9} \] This proves the assertion, including a finite upper bound rather than only degreewise constructibility. \(\square\)

Where the proper-support input is already available. Suppose \(b\) is proper between separated finite-type schemes over a field \(k\), and \(\Lambda\) is finite and killed by a power of a prime \(\ell\) invertible in \(k\). For bounded constructible \(C\) on \(W\), properness and composition of proper supports give \[ Rb_*u_!C=Rb_!u_!C=R(bu)_!C. \tag{E.C10} \] Constructible complexes, Proposition 3.2 proves that the last object is bounded constructible at precisely this field and coefficient scope. If the fibres of \(bu\) have dimension at most \(d\), it lies in \([c_0,c_1+2d]\), giving a particular interval for \(A\) in (E.C8). This invocation uses proper-support finiteness, whose proof precedes the ordinary-image argument, not constructibility of the unknown \(K\). At a more general Noetherian parameter base or different coefficient range, the corresponding proper-support finiteness theorem must be supplied at that scope; the field theorem is not silently enlarged.

Position in the generic-base-change proof. For \(K=Rj_*F\), E.2f supplies bounded constructibility on the good open, under its stated lower-dimensional induction hypothesis. E.2i shows exactly how that conclusion and the two whole-space finiteness inputs in (E.C5) recover bounded constructibility on the finite complement. Those inputs must be proved for \(Rb_*Rj_*F=R(bj)_*F\) and \(Rb_*u_!u^*Rj_*F\) in the chosen parameter-base and coefficient setting. The criterion does not prove them by naming them. Separately, E.2d and E.2g handle the comparison map once the whole-space comparison in E.2d assumption 3 is established. Keeping these steps separate avoids replacing finiteness by base change, or mistaking geometric-fibre comparison for arbitrary-base comparison. E.2m below assembles the lower-dimensional induction and the whole-space finiteness/comparison at the full ordinary left-Noetherian torsion-coefficient scope. E.2n below supplies the uniform adic/rational passage using one first reduction.

The existing field-scope whole-space finiteness inputs

Corollary E.2j (reuse the proved ordinary-image theorem). Let \(k\) be a field, let \(\ell\) be invertible in \(k\), and let \(\Lambda\) be a finite commutative ring killed by a power of \(\ell\). Suppose \(X,Y,S\) are separated finite-type \(k\)-schemes, \(j:X\hookrightarrow Y\) is open, \(b:Y\to S\) is a morphism, and \(F\in D^b_c(X,\Lambda)\). Then both \[ K=Rj_*F\in D^b_c(Y,\Lambda),\qquad B=Rb_*K=R(bj)_*F\in D^b_c(S,\Lambda). \tag{E.W1} \] No smoothness of the schemes, perfection of \(k\), or freeness of the finite coefficient modules is required. If \(b\) is proper, the good-open proper-support term in E.2i is also bounded constructible at this scope. These field-scope finiteness statements do not require the broader generic-base-change conclusions of E.2m and E.2n.

Proof and exact provider. Constructible complexes, Appendix M, Theorem M.4 proves ordinary-image bounded constructibility for every separated finite-type morphism over a field. Its initial coefficient convention explicitly includes finite rings of order invertible in the field, not only free modules over \(\mathbf Z/\ell^m\). Apply it to \(j\) and \(bj\). Both are separated and of finite type: the schemes are Noetherian and separated of finite type over \(k\), an open immersion here is quasi-compact, and graphs give the stated relative separation and finite-type properties. Composition of ordinary derived direct images identifies the actual \(Rb_*Rj_*F\) with \(R(bj)_*F\); it follows from the exact inverse-image adjunction and preservation of injectives, as used in E.2c.

The proof order in M matters. M.1 gives a finite cohomological bound and field descent first. M.2 constructs finite-support quotients without assuming the unknown sheaf constructible. M.3 recovers constructibility from finite global cohomology on a proper scheme. M.4 uses those results in its dimension induction, establishes the smooth-open case before applying induction to the constructible cone, and invokes compact-support finiteness and smooth duality for the global term. It does not assume generic base change over an arbitrary parameter scheme. The corollary uses these results together with their exact geometric and coefficient prerequisites.

The bound in M.1 is also usable here. On each member \(S_t\) of a finite affine cover of \(S\), cover \((bj)^{-1}(S_t)\) by \(r_t\) affine opens of dimension at most \(D_t\). Ignore empty inverse images. Set \(m=\max_t(D_t+r_t-1)\), or \(m=0\) if \(X\) is empty. Affine intersections and the finite Cech-to-derived-image sequence give \(R^q(bj)_*=0\) for \(q>m\). Consequently, if \(F\) is supported in cohomological degrees \([f_0,f_1]\), then \[ B\in D^{[f_0,f_1+m]}(S,\Lambda). \tag{E.W2} \] The finite cohomology tower of \(F\) gives this bound from the sheaf bound; no finite Galois cohomological dimension of \(k\) is assumed.

For the last assertion, in the notation of E.2i, restriction of the already constructible \(K\) makes \(C=u^*K\) bounded constructible. Properness of \(b\) gives \(A=Rb_*u_!C=R(bu)_!C\), and Proposition 3.2 proves its bounded constructibility, with exactly the field and finite invertible-primary coefficient hypotheses stated above. M.4 already proves constructibility of \(K\) itself in this field case; E.2i is useful for the more general residual argument, not needed to prove the same field case twice. \(\square\)

Finiteness is not arbitrary-base comparison. Even at the preceding field scope, a bounded constructible ordinary image need not commute with a specified closed-fibre base change. Take \(j:\mathbf G_{m,k}\hookrightarrow\mathbf A^1_k\), with \(k\) algebraically closed, and the constant sheaf \(\mathbf Z/\ell\). At a geometric origin, the punctured strict-local trait is the spectrum of a field and is connected. Thus the stalk of \(j_*(\mathbf Z/\ell)\) there is \(\mathbf Z/\ell\). The source of the base-changed open immersion is empty, so its direct image is zero. The degree-zero comparison is therefore \[ \mathbf Z/\ell\longrightarrow0, \tag{E.W3} \] not an isomorphism. The constant-section calculation follows from connectedness of the punctured trait; no higher cohomology computation is needed. Removing the origin gives a dense open on which this example's comparison does hold, so it does not contradict a generic theorem after shrinking.

E.2j resolves field-scope finite-coefficient finiteness, not the whole-space arbitrary-base comparison in E.2d assumption 3. For a general Noetherian parameter base, the compact-support and smooth finite-coefficient inputs are supplied by E.2k below; E.2m below assembles the remaining ordinary-image reductions at the original left-Noetherian coefficient scope. E.2n supplies the distinct uniform adic/rational comparison; M.4 is not silently promoted to that scope.

General-base proper supports and the smooth whole-space input

Proposition E.2k (existing general-base providers and their comparison). There are two distinct inputs.

  1. Let \(b:Y\to S\) be proper of finite presentation between quasi-compact, quasi-separated schemes, let \(u:W\hookrightarrow Y\) be a quasi-compact open immersion, and let \(\Lambda\) be a commutative Noetherian torsion ring. If \(C\in D^b_c(W,\Lambda)\), then the proper-support term \(A=Rb_*u_!C\) of E.2i is bounded constructible. If \(C\) has degrees \([c_0,c_1]\) and the fibres of \(bu\) have dimension at most \(e\), then \[ A=R(bu)_!C\in D^{[c_0,c_1+2e]}_c(S,\Lambda). \tag{E.WS1} \] Its compact-support base-change map is an isomorphism for every base change. In the residual comparison, E.2g supplies the proper ordinary-image comparison as well. No assumption that the unknown \(K\) is already constructible is used: the input is precisely constructibility of its known restriction \(C=u^*K\).

  2. Let \(S\) be Noetherian, let \(h:X\to S\) be separated smooth of finite presentation and pure relative dimension \(d\), and let \(\Lambda=\mathbf Z/n\), where \(n>1\) is invertible on \(S\). Let \(F\) be a finite locally constant \(\Lambda\)-module sheaf, not necessarily free. There is a dense open \(V\subset S\) such that every \(R^ih_*F|_V\) is finite locally constant, vanishes outside \(0\leq i\leq2d\), and commutes with every base change \(g:T\to V\) through the canonical ordinary-image map. Thus, for \(h=bj\) in this smooth/lisse case, E.2d assumption 3 is available after one shrinking of the parameter base. This assertion concerns arbitrary \(T\to V\), not just geometric fibres.

Proof of the proper-support assertion. The composite \(bu\) is separated of finite presentation. The quasi-compact open immersion has finite presentation, and properness identifies \(Rb_*\) with \(Rb_!\). Composition gives the equality in (E.WS1). Compact supports, Theorem 12.3 supplies constructibility on a general quasi-compact, quasi-separated base; Theorem 12.1 gives the stated interval. A uniform finite bound on fibre dimension exists for a finite-presentation morphism over a quasi-compact base, by finite affine presentations. The compact-support comparison is the one constructed in Section 9, with its composition compatibility. These providers do not have a field-only restriction. Over a general Noetherian ring, constructibility means finitely generated module stalks; it does not assert finite underlying sets.

Theorem 12.3 gives its affine-factorization, finite-cover and finite-truncation proof explicitly. Its geometric prerequisite is the AI Integrated Stacks affine-line constructibility proof, with the constant and locally constant curve reductions linked there. The corresponding native source is more-etale.tex, revision 565b10e987aba5969b21145a0833f42d69f96790, labels lemma-constructible-shriek-rel-dim-1, lemma-loc-constant-shriek-rel-dim-1, lemma-constant-shriek-rel-dim-1, and the two proper-smooth-curve lemmas. The compact-support deduction requires the curve results at the hypotheses of those lemmas.

Finite-module duality, including nonfree fibres. Write \(D_ZE=R\mathcal Hom_Z(E,\Lambda)\). The ring \(\Lambda\) is injective as a module over itself. Indeed, every ideal is \((a)\) for a divisor \(a\mid n\). The image \(x\) of \(a\) under an ideal homomorphism must satisfy \((n/a)x=0\); in \(\mathbf Z/n\) this says \(x=ay\). Multiplication by \(y\) extends the homomorphism, proving injectivity by the ideal criterion. For a finite module, decomposition into cyclic groups of orders dividing \(n\) shows that evaluation into its double dual is an isomorphism: the dual of \(\mathbf Z/r\) is the ideal generated by \(n/r\), of order \(r\), and characters separate its elements.

These facts apply internally to a finite locally constant sheaf. On an étale cover trivializing it, sheafify a resolution by finite free \(\Lambda\)-modules. Such a resolution exists degree by degree since \(\Lambda\) is Noetherian; it need not be bounded. Internal Hom from each finite free term is exact, and the resulting Ext sheaves are the constant sheaves associated to module Ext. Injectivity of \(\Lambda\) makes the positive Ext groups vanish. This establishes both exact duality and bidual evaluation locally and hence globally. In particular, for a bounded complex \(Q\) with finite locally constant cohomology, \[ H^r(D_ZQ)=\mathcal Hom_Z(H^{-r}(Q),\Lambda). \tag{E.WS2} \] Use its finite cohomology tower to obtain this formula; do not assert that nonfree modules have finite projective dimension. For any scheme map \(g:Z'\to Z\), the canonical map \(g^*D_ZQ\to D_{Z'}g^*Q\) is an isomorphism: the same local finite-module calculation identifies its cohomology map with pullback of ordinary duals. This proves the assertion without treating every constructible complex as perfect.

Proof of the smooth assertion. Put \(F^\vee=\mathcal Hom_X(F,\Lambda)\) and \(Q=Rh_!F^\vee\). The preceding proper-support providers, applied directly to \(h\), give \(Q\in D^{[0,2d]}_c(S,\Lambda)\). Choose a dense open \(V\) on which all its finitely many cohomology sheaves are locally constant. Such a common open exists by their finite constructible stratifications: take a neighbourhood of each generic point in its stratum, exclude intersections with the other irreducible components, and take the union of these finitely many opens. Nonreduced structure does not change local constancy on the étale site. Only finitely many degrees are involved, so this is not an infinite intersection of dense opens.

Relative smooth duality, Theorem 9.1 gives \(Rh^!\Lambda=\Lambda(d)[2d]\) over the parameter base. Its internal adjunction, (8.4), applied to \(F^\vee\), then gives \[ Rh_*F\simeq D_SQ(-d)[-2d]. \tag{E.WS3} \] Here bidual evaluation identifies the internal Hom on \(X\); the invertible twist can be pulled through ordinary direct image, as checked on an étale cover trivializing it. The companion constructs the trace and the smooth formula on general bases, not merely varieties over a field. Its Sections 3–6 use bounded derived trace systems and constructible compact supports before Section 9; the general compact-support input is not deduced from the smooth duality being proved.

Restrict (E.WS3) to \(V\), and apply (E.WS2). This gives \[ R^ih_*F|_V\simeq \mathcal Hom_V\bigl(H^{2d-i}(Q)|_V,\Lambda\bigr)(-d). \tag{E.WS4} \] It proves both local constancy and the asserted vanishing range, including nonfree coefficient fibres.

It remains to identify the map, rather than only the isomorphism classes. For \(g:T\to V\), write \(h_T:X_T\to T\) and \(F_T=g_X^*F\). Compact base change identifies \(g^*(Q|_V)\) with \(Rh_{T,!}F_T^\vee\). Pullback commutes with the dual of \(Q|_V\) by (E.WS2). Compose these comparisons with (E.WS3) for \(h_T\); the resulting isomorphism is \[ g^*(Rh_*F|_V)\longrightarrow Rh_{T,*}F_T. \tag{E.WS5} \] To verify it is the canonical ordinary-image comparison, recall that (8.4) is evaluation followed by the trace counit. Pulling that pairing to \(T\), then using compact base change, gives the evaluation-and-trace pairing for \(h_T\): evaluation commutes with inverse image, and the trace and its composition identities commute with base change by the companion's Section 2 and Theorem 9.1. Transposing the same pairing therefore gives the map induced by the pulled ordinary adjunction counit, which defines the canonical comparison. Equivalently, after composing with the two duality isomorphisms, both maps are the canonical pullback-of-internal-Hom map followed by the inverse dual of compact base change. Thus (E.WS5) is that map and is invertible. When \(T\) is not quasi-compact or quasi-separated, make this verification on affine opens of \(T\); their base-changed smooth morphisms retain finite presentation, and the comparisons glue by their counit definition. \(\square\)

Coefficient scope. Part 1 gives the proper-support input for Noetherian coefficients. Part 2 supplies the smooth finite locally constant \(\mathbf Z/n\)-coefficient whole-space comparison after shrinking. The finiteness exposition in SGA \(4\frac12\), Theorem 1.9 and proof Sections 2.1–2.2, treats the broader Noetherian-coefficient reduction; Part 2 treats only the finite-coefficient stage. E.2m below supplies the ordered lower-dimensional and cone arguments for general constructible ordinary coefficients; E.2l below supplies the general Noetherian-coefficient smooth reduction, its finite-monodromy argument and a uniform degree bound. Adic/rational passage still requires its own uniform justification. The general adic/rational comparison is the subject of E.2n; it uses uniform coefficient maps on one dense open.

Smooth generic comparison with Noetherian coefficients

Proposition E.2l (the full ordinary-coefficient smooth stage). Let \(S\) be Noetherian, let \(h:X\to S\) be smooth of finite type, and let \(A\) be a unital left-Noetherian ring killed by an integer \(n>0\) invertible on \(S\). Let \(F\) be a locally constant sheaf of finitely generated left \(A\)-modules on \(X_{\mathrm{\acute et}}\). Choose an upper bound \(d\) for the relative dimensions of \(h\). There is a dense open \(V\subset S\) such that \[ \begin{gathered} R^ih_*F|_V\text{ is locally constant,}\\ \text{with finitely generated left }A\text{-module stalks},\\ R^ih_*F|_V=0\quad(i>2d),\\ g^*(R^ih_*F|_V)\xrightarrow{\sim}R^ih_{T*}g_X^*F \quad(g:T\to V,\ i\geq0). \end{gathered} \tag{E.NC1} \] The last arrow is the canonical base-change map. Neither commutativity of \(A\), finiteness of its underlying set, nor separatedness of \(h\) is required here. This is an ordinary torsion statement, not an adic assertion. Smoothness over the Noetherian base makes the finite-type maps and the open restrictions used below finitely presented.

Coefficient conventions and exact operations. All direct images in this proof are derived in left-module sheaves. They agree after forgetting coefficients with abelian-sheaf direct images. To check the needed extension of E.2g to a noncommutative \(A\), use its augmented hypercover complex with free left \(A\)-module sheaves. Local contraction of each finite-support cycle is still valid, since its differential uses only integers and left-linear maps. Hom into an injective left-module sheaf makes that augmented complex exact. The BB.3 acyclicity proof, with BA.5's finite-family hypercovers, therefore computes the underlying abelian cohomology too. Apply the same argument on the qcqs inverse images of affine étale test objects. This identifies the higher direct images and their restriction and connecting maps. It does not assert that forgetting an arbitrary coefficient action preserves injectives.

Finitely generated locally constant left \(A\)-module sheaves are closed under kernels, cokernels and extensions. The proof of Constructible sheaves, Lemma 3.1 uses finite presentations and left-linear relations, so does not need commutativity for these three claims. Explicitly, trivialize two modules and the images of a finite generating set to make a map constant; its kernel is finitely generated by left-Noetherianity. For an extension, locally lift generators of a finite presentation of the quotient. There are finitely many generating relations, whose images in the constant submodule can also be made constant. The quotient presentation of the extension is then constant. No tensor product over a noncommutative \(A\) is being used.

We may work over finitely many pairwise disjoint affine neighbourhoods of the generic points of \(S_{\mathrm{red}}\), after removing intersections of its irreducible components. Their union is dense. The equivalence of étale topoi for a nilpotent thickening transports the entire comparison diagram, including its direct-image counit: for an arbitrary \(T\to S\), the pullback of \(S_{\mathrm{red}}\hookrightarrow S\) is again nilpotent. Thus results over this reduced dense open transfer to the corresponding open of \(S\). We first suppose \(S\) affine and integral, with generic point \(\eta\), and \(h\) separated and of pure relative dimension \(d\). No regularity or normality of the parameter base is assumed.

A finite trivializing cover without finite module fibres. We explain the geometric step for any of these coefficient rings. A connected component \(T_0\) of the smooth generic fibre \(X_\eta\) is normal and integral. Indeed smooth étale charts reduce normality to a polynomial ring over a field and the integral-closure identity in AW.2; normal Noetherian components are disjoint. Write \(K\) for its function field and \(M\) for a geometric generic stalk of \(F\). The field-topos equivalence AB.2 applies to arbitrary discrete stalks, not just finite sets. It gives a continuous \(A\)-linear action of \(\operatorname{Gal}(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K)\) on \(M\). Intersect the open stabilizers of finitely many \(A\)-generators of \(M\). This intersection is the kernel of the action on all of \(M\), hence is open and normal. Its fixed field is a finite Galois extension \(L/K\), with group \(G\).

Normalize \(T_0\) in \(L\). This normalization is finite. On a normal Noetherian affine domain \(R\subset K\), choose an integral \(K\)-basis \(e_1,\ldots,e_r\) of the separable extension \(L/K\), scaling an arbitrary basis to clear the coefficients of its monic equations. Its trace-pairing determinant \(\Delta\) is nonzero. If \(z\) is integral over \(R\), each \(\operatorname{Tr}(ze_i)\) is integral and lies in \(K\), so belongs to \(R\) by normality. The adjugate matrix bounds the coordinates of \(z\) by \(\Delta^{-1}R^r\). The integral closure is a submodule of this finite module, and is therefore finite. Localization of integral closure glues these affine normalizations.

This finite normalization is in fact étale. On an affine étale chart trivializing \(F\), take a normal connected component with function field \(K'\). Its absolute Galois group acts trivially on \(M\); after choosing a compatible embedding into \(K^{\mathrm{sep}}\), this implies \(L\subset K'\). Consequently \[ L\otimes_KK'\simeq\prod_{\sigma\in G}K'. \tag{E.NC2} \] Normalization commutes with this étale base change by AW.2. The chart is normal, so the pulled normalization is the disjoint union of \(|G|\) copies of that chart. Hence the cover is finite étale and its Galois action makes it a \(G\)-torsor, as checked on these charts. On its normal source the pulled sheaf is constant: the unit to its generic direct image is an isomorphism by AZ.4, checked on trivializing charts, and its generic sheaf is \(\underline M\). This also retains the generic descent action on \(M\).

There are only finitely many connected components of \(X_\eta\). Spread their clopen decomposition and the finitely many covers, actions, torsor isomorphisms and inverses to some common base open using BD.1–2. The trivializations and their inverses spread by AZ.3's morphism-continuity argument. For the present left modules this is the same finite-generator, finite-relation proof: sections descend, their finitely many equations eventually hold, and two inverse maps retain their inverse identities. Include the finitely many equivariance equations. Thus on each resulting clopen piece we have \[ p:X_1\longrightarrow X\text{ a finite étale }G\text{-torsor}, \qquad p^*F\simeq\underline M, \qquad h_1=hp. \tag{E.NC3} \] We prove the claim piece by piece and intersect only finitely many base opens. The finite-set classification in Appendix N alone would not justify this step for an infinite underlying \(M\). The argument above supplies that missing distinction. No condition that \(|G|\) be invertible is imposed.

Prime coefficients and descent. First suppose \(\ell A=0\) for one prime \(\ell\) invertible on \(S\). Write the \(\mathbb F_\ell\)-vector space \(M\) as the directed union of its finite-dimensional subspaces. For a finite-dimensional space, a basis identifies its constant sheaf with a finite direct sum of \(\mathbb F_\ell\). Fixed-scheme filtered-coefficient continuity in BA.5, on the qcqs inverse images of affine étale objects, now gives the natural isomorphism \[ R^qh_{1*}\underline M\simeq (R^qh_{1*}\mathbb F_\ell)\otimes_{\mathbb F_\ell}M. \tag{E.NC4} \] It is independent of the chosen finite-dimensional bases, being induced by the natural coefficient maps. Thus it respects the \(A\)-action, deck descent and base-change comparisons. The finite-dimensional subspaces need not be \(A\)-submodules; their union and this natural map recover the full action.

Apply E.2k to the separated smooth \(h_1\) and \(\mathbb F_\ell\). On one dense open, all \(R^qh_{1*}\mathbb F_\ell\) are finite locally constant, vanish for \(q>2d\), and commute with every base change. Formula (E.NC4) makes each nonzero cover term a locally constant finitely generated \(A\)-module sheaf: locally it is a finite direct sum of \(M\).

The augmented Čech nerve of \(p\), with coefficients \(F\), gives \[ E_2^{a,b}=\mathcal H^a\bigl(G,R^bh_{1*}\underline M\bigr) \ \Longrightarrow\ R^{a+b}h_*F. \tag{E.NC5} \] Here \(\mathcal H^a(G,-)\) means cohomology of the group-cochain complex of sheaves, with its descent action. To construct the sequence, the augmented nerve is exact on geometric stalks: choose one lift through the finite étale surjection and insert it to contract the augmented complex. Finite étale direct image is exact, so applying derived \(h_*\) to this resolution gives the first-quadrant sequence. The torsor identification makes degree \(a\) the product indexed by the finite set \(G^a\), with the usual alternating group differential. These are finite products of the cover cohomology sheaves.

Every \(E_2^{a,b}\), subsequent subquotient, and abutment is therefore locally constant and finitely generated over \(A\), by the exact operations proved above. For a fixed total degree only finitely many positions contribute, so the filtration and the spectral-sequence comparison are finite in that degree, although the group-cochain complex may be unbounded. Exact inverse image commutes with its finite products and cohomology. The cover-term base-change maps yield an isomorphism of the spectral sequences and hence of their abutments. Their augmentation is the original section restriction, so this abutment map is the canonical ordinary-image comparison, not an unrelated isomorphism. The same dense open works for all degrees; no further open was chosen for individual values of \(a\).

Why the bound is uniform. One cannot obtain an upper bound by claiming that finite-group cohomology vanishes above a fixed degree: this is false when \(\ell\mid |G|\). Instead use the comparison just proved for the geometric generic point \(\bar\eta\). Cohomological dimension, Corollary 7.2 applies to every torsion sheaf on a \(d\)-dimensional finite-type scheme over a separably closed field. It gives \[ (R^ih_*F)_{\bar\eta}\simeq H^i(X_{\bar\eta},F)=0 \qquad(i>2d). \tag{E.NC6} \] Each of these higher images is already locally constant on the same integral base open. A locally constant sheaf with zero generic stalk is zero there: after trivialization the zero locus is both open and closed, and the base is connected. This proves simultaneous vanishing on the previously chosen open, with no infinite intersection of dense opens. The provider's proof establishes the absolute torsion bound through affine vanishing and the étale-to-Zariski spectral sequence; compact-support vanishing alone would not suffice for this ordinary-image step.

Returning to the original ring. If \(n=1\), the ring and sheaf are zero. Otherwise the Chinese-remainder idempotents of \(\mathbf Z/n\) are central in \(A\) and decompose \(A\) and \(F\) into finitely many prime-primary parts.
